Proof. Set sn = B(2n), which tends to infinity by Lemma 9.10. Corollary 18.12 supplies every
hypothesis of Lemma 2.5, including the deterministic nature of the subsequential multiplier. The
normalization prescribed by that lemma is exactly Equation (18.5). Its conclusion proves the
proposition, with the quantum-area measure unchanged. This proves the critical FK clause of
Theorem 1.1.

    The normalization uses unconditional diameter laws and is deterministic. The proof uses the
deterministic multiplier assertion in (F1); it does not replace a random subsequential multiplier by
a deterministic one. All constants and all subsequence choices in this Section may depend on the
fixed value of q. No endpoint statement or uniformity as q approaches 0 or 4 is used.

along a subsequence, their excursion-conditioned joint laws converge along that subsequence by the

conditional kernel.

   The corresponding full-contour invariance principle has a useful direct interpretation. Given a
schedule with J first-coordinate moves, excursion conditioning makes the two coordinate strings
independent nonnegative bridges of lengths J and N − J. The conditional mass of J is

                                           2−N N

It is concentrated at J/N = 1/2. For each ϵ > 0, the probability of |J − N/2| > ϵN is at
most CN 3 e−cϵ N , by the ordinary binomial bound and (19.4). Conditional on J, all sched-

supr≤N |Jr − rJ/N |/N → 0 in probability. One way to see the last assertion is to use

changes converge uniformly to half speed. The reflection formula gives the nonnegative-bridge
finite-dimensional limits as the Brownian excursion transition densities. It also gives tightness: on a
fixed interior time interval use the bounded L2 density with respect to free increments; close to each
endpoint use the one-dimensional version   √ of (19.6) and

     By Theorem 13.2, equivalently [34, Theorem 1.1], this pair encodes the area-one quantum sphere,
with its area-parametrized space-filling SLE8 . This is the ordinary sphere of [13, Definition 4.21(ii)].
Its distinguished points can be forgotten, by [13, Proposition A.13]; we write q for the common
point η(0) = η(1) used by the encoding. The strict positivity of both excursions on (0, 1) gives

                                          η −1 ({q}) = {0, 1}.                                   (19.13)

All deterministic Brownian boundary-length conventions here are the same ones as in the bilateral
construction and Theorem 13.2.

19.2    The metric on interior sphere charts
Lemma 19.2 (Interior sphere metric). In the joint extraction with distance units BN and
deterministic multiplier Λ selected above, the limiting finite-excursion local passage rule satisfies

                                       FU (x, y) = ΛDh (x, y; U )

for every buffered chart U compactly contained in S \ {q} and every x, y joined by a path compactly
in U . This identification retains the upper realization and lower subpath-extraction conclusions of
Theorem 17.3.



                                                  224
Proof. Fix rational 0 < δ < 1/2, and write a = δ, b = 1 − δ. Retain the complete collection of
internal interval-chart passage data constructed solely from the increments on [aN , bN ]. It includes
every certificate whose range has positive clearance from the boundary of the interval surface, by
Lemma 15.2. Apply (19.11) to this collection, first in finitely many compact charts and then by
the countable extraction of Proposition 15.1. In the bilateral reference law its compact-interior
local passage rule equals ΛD, by Theorem 17.3; the equality event has probability one and is
therefore preserved by the limiting density.
    The restricted increments determine an abstract decorated quantum surface, including its
quantum boundary-length units. For the cone this is [18, Lemma 4.9]. The sphere statement needed   √
here is supplied separately by the final paragraph of the proof of [34, Theorem 1.1, the case γ ≤ 2,
p. 49]: conditionally on the contour values at δ and 1 − δ, the quantum surface complementary
to η([0, δ]) ∪ η([1 − δ, 1]) has the corresponding cone conditional law and is determined, together
with its curve, by the restricted contour. This is the symmetric interval statement recorded in
Theorem 13.2. Its quantum units agree with those in the density calculation. Metric locality and
conformal covariance therefore identify its internal D with the internal metric on its image in
the area-one sphere. The transported equality is an equality with this sphere’s Dh in the fixed
normalization.
    This equality is an event of the restricted contour and its passage data, since those contour
increments determine the abstract interval surface. Adjoining the outside contour therefore
preserves the same probability-one equality.
    Finally, if K ⊂ S \ {q} is compact, continuity of η and (19.13) imply that η −1 (K) is contained
in (δ, 1 − δ) for some rational 0 < δ < 1/2. Choose a smaller positive rational δ if necessary. The
image of the omitted compact time intervals is disjoint from K, so K lies in the interior of the
interval surface and has positive spatial clearance from its boundary. Its local certificates are
therefore among the ones just transferred. By a countable exhaustion, the local passage rule off q
equals ΛDh simultaneously in these charts, with the same deterministic Λ.


    We also record how the transfer behaves when the complete outside contour is retained. The
following conditional-kernel statement allows the inside passage observations to be used jointly
with outside contour observations.

Lemma 19.3 (The kernel after adjoining the outside contour). Fix 0 < a < b < 1. In the setup
of Equation (19.11), suppose the free joint limit of the block increments WN and block-measurable
passage data HN disintegrates as µ(dw)K(w, dz). Write W, H for the corresponding interior
increment path and passage datum in their joint excursion-conditioned limit with the complete
contour. The conditional law of H given that contour is K(W, ·).

Proof. The assertion follows from the Markov factorization: given the entrance height x and
the inside increments, the outside consists of a killed entrance bridge and a killed exit bridge,
independent of the inside data. Their weights are the two factors in (19.5). On compact positive-
height ranges, the reflection formula for qm (x, y) (the four coordinate reflections of the free
two-dimensional kernel) gives uniform bridge finite-dimensional limits. For tightness at the zero
                                                            (1)
endpoint, the one-dimensional reflection formula gives qr (u, v) ≤ C(u + 1)(v + 1)(r + 1)−3/2 :
telescope step-two differences of pr in the smaller of u, v, using symmetry of the kernel and the
binomial Gaussian bound. Averaging over schedules    √ as in (19.8) consequently gives qm (y, z) ≤
CR (y1 + 1)(y2 + 1)N −2 when m ≍ N and |z| ≤ R N . On a fixed positive-height compact set,
qaN (0, z) ≥ cR N −2 , so the proof of (19.6) applies to the small dyadic blocks of this entrance

19.3     Endpoint diameters
The density estimate controls the metric information which an interior change of measure cannot
see. Let vi be the primal vertex at contour time i, with v0 = vN , and set

                         TN (δ) = {vi : 0 ≤ i ≤ ⌊δN ⌋ or N − ⌊δN ⌋ ≤ i ≤ N }.

For 1 ≤ l ≤ N/4, Cauchy–Schwarz, (19.6), and (19.2) give
                           h                i
                          −1                        −1
                    Eexc
                     N   BN  D([l, 2l]) ≤ ∥fN,l ∥2 BN  D([0, l])                   ≤ C(l/N )s .       (19.14)
                                                                              2

The internal distances in this display are genuine functions of the block increments, so the density
calculation applies to them exactly. If only endpoint norms are used in Theorem 17.3, the diameter
bound follows by balanced binary subdivision: at depth r, the maximum of at most 2r endpoint
costs has Lk norm at most C(l/N )s 2−r(s−1/k) in BN units; summing these bounds is finite since
s > 1/k. Rounded subdivision lengths change the constant only.
    For 0 < δ ≤ 1/8 and δN ≥ 1, choose J with 2J ≤ δN < 2J+1 . The chain

                                    [0, 1], [1, 2], [2, 4], . . . , [2J , 2J+1 ]

covers [0, δN ] and its consecutive intervals share endpoints. Every block except [0, 1] in this chain
satisfies the hypothesis of (19.14). Its first interval has graph diameter at most one. Connect
points through the common endpoints using the interval paths and sum the resulting diameter
bounds. Time reversal gives the same bound at N , and the two chains meet at v0 = vN . If δN < 1,
the endpoint set consists only of this vertex. We have proved

This is an estimate for the actual ambient graph metric; the internal metrics appeared only to

    On a closed interior time interval, the uniform L2 density in Lemma 19.1 transfers the free
time-modulus estimate of Lemma 14.2. Explicitly, if an event has free probability r, its conditioned
                           √
probability is at most Ca,b r. Together with (19.16), this gives tightness of the cyclic time modulus
of the rescaled ambient distances on [0, 1]. Indeed first cover [δ, 1 − δ] by finitely many interior
blocks, and then join its ends to the two endpoint chains just constructed. The same argument
bounds the full diameter in probability.

19.4    Identification of unrestricted distances
Lemma 19.4 (The measure counted by the tree tour). Let vi be the primal vertex represented by
contour time i, with vN = v0 . Then, for every v ∈ V (Mn ),

                                 #{0 ≤ i < N : vi = v} = degMn (v).                          (19.17)

Consequently, if
                           1   X
                   µn =               degMn (v) δv ,        fn (t) = v⌊N t⌋ ,   0 ≤ t ≤ 1,
                          2n v∈V (M )
                                    n


then (fn )∗ Leb[0,1] = µn . Conditional on the map, independent uniform times give independent
samples from µn .
Proof. Associate time slot i ∈ {0, . . . , N − 1} with the step departing at i. The two horizontal
traversals of a tree edge depart once from each endpoint, so these slots count its two incidences.
Every vertical step belongs to a unique matched pair with terminal times u < v. By (13.3), this
pair adds a non-tree edge between vu−1 and vv−1 ; its two departure slots count exactly those two
incidences. This also counts a loop twice. Every slot belongs to exactly one such pair, proving
(19.17). Each slot has time mass 1/N = 1/(2n), which proves the pushforward identity and the
sampling assertion.

Proposition 19.5 (Sphere identification). For every sequence of positive integers n → ∞, there
is a further subsequence and a deterministic Λ ∈ (0, ∞) such that
                                           −1
                                (V (Mn ), B2n dn , µn ) ⇒ (S, ΛDh , µh )                     (19.18)

in the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov topology. The constants Λ lie in a fixed deterministic compact
subset of (0, ∞).
Proof. Choose a simultaneous bilateral extraction as in Theorem 17.3, with its deterministic
multiplier Λ. Lemma 19.2 gives convergent sphere certificates on every compact subset of S \ {q},
with local passage rule ΛDh . The local certificate assertions include realization with open margins,
subpath extraction, and uniformly negligible costs for joining nearby endpoints.
   We give the compactness and path argument explicitly. Put
                                       −1
                          ρN (t, u) = BN  dn (v⌊N t⌋ , v⌊N u⌋ ),       0 ≤ t, u ≤ 1.

                                                     227
Consecutive contour vertices are equal or joined by a tree edge, so replacing ρN by its bilinear
interpolation changes it by at most 2/BN . The cyclic time modulus and diameter bound proved
above imply tightness of these interpolations in C([0, 1]2 ). Extract jointly with the contours and
all certificate charts, and use an almost sure representation. Then ρN converges uniformly to a
continuous pseudometric ρ on [0, 1], with ρ(0, 1) = 0. The limiting contour determines the sphere
and its continuous surjection η. In particular the endpoint time sets have limiting ρ-diameters
tending to zero. We will show that
                             ρ(t, u) = ΛDh (η(t), η(u))      (0 ≤ t, u ≤ 1).                    (19.19)
    The projection part of Proposition 15.1 applies to the finite excursion sewing as well: its proof
uses uniform contour convergence and the defining link relations. Thus all representatives of a
single vertex have Dh -close images, uniformly over vertices, and the two endpoints of every graph
edge have Dh -distance tending to zero, uniformly over edges. To see the uniform assertion directly,
a contrary sequence has convergent contour times; the limiting tree identification or matched
second-coordinate chord identifies the two images. This also covers links involving time 0 or 1,
which project to q. Choose any representative of each vertex and write πN (v) for its image under η
at the rescaled time. Let εN → 0 bound both representative errors and edge-image jumps. These
maps are only used to locate paths in the limiting surface; all costs remain discrete all-edge costs.
    We first record a uniform local crossing fact. If K ⊂ S \ {q} is compact, take finitely many
pairs of open sets Vi ⋐ Ui covering K, all contained in interval-surface interiors. Choose r > 0 so
small that every Dh -ball of radius 3r about a point of K is contained in one of the Ui with positive
clearance from its boundary. A discrete path beginning at such a point, stopped on first reaching
Dh -displacement r, stays in that compact chart after an εN enlargement. For all sufficiently large
N , it has scaled cost at least Λr/2. Otherwise a subsequence of these paths has bounded cost and
compact confinement; subpath extraction gives a certificate with endpoints at distance r and cost
at most Λr/2, contradicting the local lower comparison. Compactness of K and the finite cover
make this assertion uniform in its starting point and in the path. Paths of larger cost already
satisfy it. It follows that a path of scaled cost at most L can be divided into at most 2L/(Λr) + 2
such steps, with a final shorter step, as long as it stays in K.
    For the upper bound in (19.19), fix t, u and a Dh -geodesic P from x = η(t) to y = η(u). A
compact subarc disjoint from q has a finite subdivision into pieces lying with open margins in
the sets Ui of a finite local cover. For each piece the local upper comparison and realization
supply approximating discrete paths with cost at most Λ times that piece’s Dh -length, plus an
arbitrarily prescribed positive error. Uniform joining connects their endpoints, also with vanishing
cost. Consequently a geodesic whose range avoids q gives ρ(t, u) ≤ ΛDh (x, y).
    Here is the endpoint modification when P meets q, also valid if one or both endpoints are q.
Given δ > 0, compactness and (19.13) give a radius rδ > 0 such that
                                η −1 (B Dh (q, 2rδ )) ⊂ [0, δ) ∪ (1 − δ, 1].                    (19.20)
If P enters BDh (q, rδ ), retain its portion before the first entry and its portion after the last exit.
Both are compactly away from q except for their retained endpoints, which are still at positive
distance from q unless the whole portion is empty. Apply the finite local subdivision to these
portions. The two cut endpoints have contour representatives in the endpoint time set of (19.20).
Their approximating vertices can be connected at cost at most the scaled diameter of TN (δ), with
a vanishing joining error; the same applies if an original endpoint lies in this set. The sum of the
retained geodesic lengths is at most Dh (x, y). Uniform convergence of ρN therefore gives
                           ρ(t, u) ≤ ΛDh (x, y) + diamρ ([0, δ] ∪ [1 − δ, 1]).

                                                   228
The last term tends to zero by continuity of ρ at 0 and 1. This proves the upper bound in all cases.
     For the lower bound, choose shortest discrete paths between the two contour vertices. Their
scaled lengths converge to ρ(t, u) and are bounded. Fix r > 0. If a path has vertices whose images
lie in BDh (q, r), remove the portion between its first and last such vertices, retaining its prefix
and suffix. The removed portion has nonnegative cost. Its two cut endpoints have Dh -distance at
most 2r. Apart from these endpoints, the retained pieces stay outside BDh (q, r); since edge-image
jumps are at most εN , they are contained in S \ BDh (q, r/2) for large N . If there is no visit, retain
the entire path. Cases with an endpoint inside the ball simply have an empty retained piece.
     Cover S \ BDh (q, r/2) by the finite buffered local charts above and choose a fixed step size
r > 0 for that cover. Divide each retained piece at successive first Dh -displacements r′ . The
  ′

uniform crossing fact bounds the number of pieces independently of N . Pass to a further extraction
recording their endpoints, costs, and marked subpaths. Each piece has a limiting certificate
compactly in one chart. Its limiting cost is at least Λ times its endpoints’ Dh -distance. Add these
inequalities, including the final shorter pieces. The triangle inequality in Dh then gives
                                                                  
                                     ρ(t, u) ≥ Λ Dh (x, y) − 2r .

No cost is assigned to the removed segment in this bound. Letting r ↓ 0 proves the required lower
inequality. In particular, it rules out a short global path escaping a local chart and returning at a
different point; such a path has paid for its successive chart crossings. We have proved (19.19).
    The construction applies first on a countable dense set of contour pairs. Continuity of ρ, Dh ,
and η extends it to every pair. As ρN → ρ uniformly, the correspondence

                                  RN = {(v⌊N t⌋ , η(t)) : 0 ≤ t ≤ 1}
                                                             −1
has distortion tending to zero when the discrete metric is BN   dn and the sphere metric is ΛDh .
It covers both spaces: every primal vertex has a contour visit and η is surjective. Moreover,
Lemma 19.4 gives (fn )∗ Leb = µn , and quantum-area parametrization gives η∗ Leb = µh . Lemma 2.3
therefore bounds the GHP distance by half the same distortion. This proves (19.18). The bounds
and deterministic nature of Λ come from Theorem 17.3; a change of density cannot change a fixed
almost sure equality constant.

19.5    A deterministic normalization for every integer
Proof of the spanning-tree case of Theorem 1.1. Let X = diamDh S. The ordinary area-one quan-
tum sphere is compact and nontrivial, so 0 < X < ∞ almost surely. With the center m from
Lemma 2.4, define, for every positive integer n,
                                                                                   
                Xn = diamdn V (Mn ),        an = exp m(log X) − m log(Xn ∨ 1)           .       (19.21)

These are positive deterministic constants; the truncation includes one-vertex maps. Proposi-
tion 19.5 supplies every hypothesis of Lemma 2.5, with sn = B2n → ∞ and a deterministic positive
subsequential multiplier Λ. Hence an → 0 and

                                 (V (Mn ), an dn , µn ) =⇒ (S, Dh , µh )

in GHP topology along all positive integers. All distances use every primal edge, and forgetting
the root and spanning tree gives the unmarked metric probability spaces in the theorem.
